# SOURCE_FILE: CHEY-2024-04-02_TotalExtraction_13506528.pdf
Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Management 
Emergency Operations Plan Summary 
Albin, Burns, Cheyenne, Pine Bluffs 
 
This plan is the revision of what was previously titled “Emergency Response Plan”.  Necessary 
changes were put in the plan so EMA would be more in line with State Statutes and the National 
Response Framework.  The new integrated Emergency Operations Plan for Laramie County and 
the cities/towns therein as a City - County Program.  This plan supersedes any previous 
emergency management/civil defense plans promulgated by the county for this purpose.  It 
provides a frame-work in which the departments of each city, town and the county can plan and 
perform their respective emergency functions during a disaster or national emergency. 
This plan recognizes the need of ongoing Emergency Management Planning and Coordination 
by all jurisdictions of government within Laramie County.  This plan attempts to be all inclusive in 
combining the phases of Emergency Management, which are: (1) Mitigation, (2) Preparedness, 
(3) Response, (4) Recovery, and (5) Prevention.  
 
In summary, the plan covers the following:  
Base Plan  
Introduction to the plan 
Vulnerabilities 
Roles and Responsibilities for Senior Officials 
Emergency Operations Center Operations – leadership & activation protocols 
Public Information & Warning 
Emergency and Disaster Assistance – State and Federal process 
 
Emergency Support Functions (ESF) 
This section defines and describes the coordination for the 15 ESF’s (The Means), Lifelines (The 
Ends) and Core Capabilities (The Ways) in which we coordinate with all community partners 
during all levels of EOC activation for emergency response, Planning, and Training needs.  
 
Incident Support Annex 
The Incident Annex identifies and outlines specific incidents that have the highest probability of 
occurring within Laramie County.   Each annex provides a description of each event, the effects 
it could potentially have within the county and an outline of roles, responsibilities, and coordinating 
efforts for each type of event.   
Support Annexes 
The Support Annex Assistance Guide outlines specific tasks or functions that may be carried out 
during and after an emergency or a disaster. The guide describes, in specific detail, the 
operational system identified in the Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan that 
will be used to carry out the functions listed.  
Planning Annexes 
The Planning Annex identifies specific plans within the ERP that may be utilized and followed 
during and after an emergency or a disaster. These annexes are designed to be the major support 
system for the EOC. These planning annexes play an important part in the needs of the affected 
jurisdiction to provide logistics, coordination, documentation, and help provide additional support 
to the incident.

PRIVACY STATEMENT 
The disclosure of information in this plan could compromise the security of essential 
equipment, services, and systems of Laramie County or otherwise in part Laramie 
County’s ability to carry out essential emergency responsibilities.  Distribution of this plan 
in its entirety is limited to those associates who need to know the information in order to 
successfully activate and implement the Plan. 
Due to the compilation of potentially sensitive data, this plan should be protected under 
the provisions of Wyo. Stat. §16-4-203(b)(vi). Portions of this Plan contain information 
that raises personal privacy or other concerns, and those portions may be exempt from 
mandatory disclosure under the Freedom of Information Act.  See 5 U.S.C. §552, 41 
C.F.R. Part 105-60. 
Any decision to disclose information in this plan outside of Laramie County or to withhold 
information in this Plan from a non-Laramie County requester must be coordinated with 
the Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Management Director and the Laramie 
County Attorney’s Office.

Emergency Operations Plan (EOP) 
 
 
Effective Date: January 2024 
 
To All Recipients: 
 
 
 
 
 
Transmitted herewith is the new integrated Emergency Operations Plan (base plan, emergency 
support functions, incident annexes, support annexes, and planning annexes) for Laramie County 
and the cities/towns therein as a City - County Program.  This plan supersedes any previous 
emergency management/civil defense plans promulgated by the county for this purpose.  It 
provides a frame-work in which the departments of each city, town and the county can plan and 
perform their respective emergency functions during a disaster or national emergency. 
This plan recognizes the need of ongoing Emergency Management Planning and Coordination 
by all jurisdictions of government within Laramie County.  This plan attempts to be all inclusive in 
combining the phases of Emergency Management, which are: (1) Mitigation, (2) Preparedness, 
(3) Response, (4) Recovery, and (5) Prevention. 
This plan is in accordance with existing Federal, State, and local statutes.  It has been approved 
by Laramie County Board of County Commissioners and the Wyoming Office of Homeland 
Security.  It will be revised and updated as required.  All recipients shall advise Cheyenne/Laramie 
County Emergency Management Agency of any change that might result in the Plan’s 
improvement, or increase its usefulness.

cases, removed due to too much information or irrelevance.  A list of more specific record 
of changes will be within the 2026 update in the table when less changes are made.

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
1 
 
 
Introduction 
The Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Management Agency takes a proactive role in 
potential disasters and emergencies, either natural or manmade, for the purposes of 
“Preparedness, Response, Recovery, Mitigation and Protection” of Laramie County and its 
citizens. This plan is designed to lessen, or in some cases, prevent loss of life and property during 
disaster situations. 
The goal of the Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan is to bring together a 
strong team of agency representatives whose early involvement in an event will expedite the 
coordination of local resources to assist an impacted area. With the assistance of this plan, the 
team coordinates the efforts of local services to restore, supply or re-supply local infrastructure 
and needs of the communities within Laramie County. 
The Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Management Agency (CLCEMA) is tasked under 
Wyoming Statute § 19-13-101 et seq. for developing and implementing a crisis operations plan 
for coordinating local response to events.  The Emergency Management Agency along with local 
first responders and emergency support functions have built this plan to assign responsibilities 
for carrying out essential support functions and event specific actions.  
This plan is updated to ensure compliance with both the “National Incident Management System” 
(NIMS) and the “National Response Framework” (NRF), but more importantly the “Wyoming 
Response Plan” (WRP). The State of Wyoming will become our closest ally in times of crisis. They 
will provide a network of availability to both state level resources and assists in federal response 
when applicable. 
This plan consists of a “Base Plan”, “Emergency Support Functions” (ESFs), “Incident Specific 
Annexes”, “Support Annexes”, and “Planning Annexes”. The Base Plan provides the initial 
“framework” for the government’s response to incident management.  It follows “the Incident 
Command System” (ICS), allowing for expansion and contraction of the organization depending 
on the needs of the responders and complexity of the incident. The ESFs (listed later in the plan) 
are used to support the ICS and provide specific needs during an incident. The Incident Specific 
Annexes are detailed to a pre-determined event, being natural or manmade. 
As a representative of government (elected or employed) or as a member of emergency 
services, it is imperative you know the content of the plan. You can and will play an 
important role in the government’s response to a large scale emergency or disaster.  Your 
preparedness is an essential part of the successful implementation and outcome of this 
plan by providing the citizens with both “Continuity of Operations” and “Continuity of 
Government”. 
 
Thank you, 
Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Management Agency 
 
 
Cheyenne/Laramie County EOP – Base Plan 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
2 
 
Purpose 
The purpose of the Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan (EOP) is to establish 
a comprehensive, local, all-hazards approach to emergency incident coordination and 
management across a spectrum of activities including prevention, preparedness, response, 
recovery and protection.  
The EOP incorporates best practices and procedures from various incident management 
disciplines—homeland security, emergency management, law enforcement, firefighting, 
hazardous materials response, public works, public health, emergency medical services, and 
responder and recovery worker health and safety—and integrates them into a unified coordinating 
structure.  
The EOP provides the framework for local interaction with other local governments, state, and 
federal governments; the private sector; and other non-governmental organizations in the context 
of domestic incident prevention, preparedness, response, and recovery activities. It describes 
capabilities and resources and establishes responsibilities, operational processes, and protocols 
to help protect Laramie County from natural and manmade hazards including terrorism; save 
lives; protect public health, safety, property, and the environment; and reduce adverse 
psychological consequences and disruptions. Finally, the EOP serves as the foundation for the 
development of detailed annexes and procedures to effectively and efficiently implement local 
incident management activities and assistance in the context of specific types of incidents.  
The EOP, using NIMS, establishes mechanisms to maximize the integration of incident-related 
prevention, preparedness, response, and recovery activities. The EOP will improve coordination 
and integration of federal, state, local, regional, private-sector, and non-governmental 
organization partners. It also maximizes efficient utilization of resources needed for effective 
incident management and critical infrastructure/key resources protection and restoration. 
Scope 
The EOP covers the full range of complex and constantly changing requirements in anticipation 
of or in response to natural and man-made disasters, emergencies, threats or acts of terrorism. 
The EOP also provides the basis to initiate long-term community recovery and mitigation 
activities. The EOP establishes interagency and multi-jurisdictional mechanisms for local 
government involvement in, and coordination of, emergency incident management operations.  
This plan distinguishes between incidents that require only local coordination vs. incidents that 
require assistance from state and federal partners.  
This plan is applicable to all local departments and agencies that may be requested to provide 
assistance or conduct operations in the context of actual or potential local emergency incidents. 
Coordination and response within this plan is in line with the interrelationships of the Emergency 
Support Functions (ESF), Core Capabilities, and Community Lifelines as identified within the 
National Response Framework. The EOP is applicable to incidents that may occur at sites under 
the control of Laramie County government and/or any jurisdiction within Laramie County.  
 
 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
3 
 
Emergency Operations Plan Applicability 
The EOP applies to all local departments and agencies that may be requested to provide 
assistance or conduct operations in actual or potential local emergency incidents. These incidents 
require a coordinated response by an appropriate combination of Federal, State, local, private-
sector, and nongovernmental entities. 
Situation & Assumptions 
Situation 
Laramie County sits on a plateau in the southeast corner of Wyoming, covering an area of 2685 
square miles. Elevation varies from 7000 feet at the western boundary to 5000 feet at the eastern 
boundary (Cheyenne-6082 ft.; Pine Bluffs-5042 ft.). The area is mostly “High Plains” grassland 
with a total of 8.3 square miles of surface water.   
Laramie County has a total population of 100,723 (2022 Census Data) with a labor force of an 
estimated 49,175. Most lands are privately owned with agriculture as the primary use. The 
assessed valuation is $1.5 Billion with total taxes levied of $102 Million.  
Average per capita income is an estimated $341,104. With three levels of government in the 
county, the majority of employment is government or government related.  
Laramie County’s demographics are quickly changing as Cheyenne and the surrounding county 
grows. Light industrial areas are growing, bringing in larger businesses. These businesses bring 
employment making residential subdivisions and business parks grow, causing both the city and 
county population to expand.  
Cheyenne is a regional transportation hub. Cheyenne is a main hub for the Union Pacific Railroad 
(UPRR) that still uses the same east-west route laid out in the 1860’s. Cheyenne sits at the 
intersection of two major interstates, I-25 and I-80, which results in large amounts of truck traffic 
flow through the county. 
The Federal Government plays a major roll for Cheyenne and Laramie County. Located adjacent 
to Cheyenne is F.E. Warren Air Force Base, a nuclear missile facility that maintains and operates 
the Minuteman missile sites located throughout the region. The Federal Courts, U.S. Marshall’s 
Office, Bureau of Land Management and Internal Revenue Service all have offices located in 
Cheyenne. 
Population centers for Laramie County include: (Estimated as of August 2020) 
Cheyenne 65,312 
 
Unincorporated 30,661 
 
Pine Bluffs 1,200 
Burns 301 
 
 
Albin 190 
Laramie County does not provide water or sewer infrastructure for most residential areas with the 
exception of the South Cheyenne Water and Sewer District. Water for this district is purchased 
from the Cheyenne Board of Public Utilities. Most county residences use private well and septic 
systems. 
County electric utilities are provided by local grids managed by a private utility company and a 
Utility Coop in the eastern portion of the county. The smaller communities of eastern Laramie 
County have limited utility infrastructure using well water systems and either waste collection 
ponds or private septic systems.    
 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
4 
 
Cheyenne’s infrastructure is multi-faceted. Cheyenne’s electricity is supplied mostly from the 
south out of Colorado supported by a redundant grid system. Water is supplied by pipeline from 
the mountains west of Laramie, Wyoming to a new water treatment plant and supplemented by 
numerous wells. Natural gas is supplied from several pipelines to a local distribution system. 
Sewer treatment facilities located east of town are secured and controlled, as is the water 
treatment plant.  
Emergency services in the City of Cheyenne and Laramie County are provided by numerous fire 
protection districts and municipal fire services, government law enforcement and both contracted 
and volunteer EMS providers. F.E. Warren AFB and the Wyoming Air National Guard also provide 
full time fire and airport protection services. Several industrial units, such as Holly Frontier Oil 
Refinery and Dyno Nobel give specific assistance to the emergency responders through mutual 
aid agreements. Both state and federal law enforcement agencies can, and do, assist locals when 
needed. 
Cheyenne and Laramie County has experienced two large scale disasters requiring assistance 
from both the State of Wyoming and the Federal Government. Both the tornado of 1979 and the 
flash flood of 1985 proved to be deadly storms that demonstrated the need for this plan. 
Assumptions 
The EOP is predicated on a realistic approach to the problems likely to be encountered in Laramie 
County during major emergencies and/or disasters, therefore: 
1. Laramie County and its cities and towns will continue to be exposed to the hazards 
identified in this plan as well as others which may develop in the future. 
2. An emergency or disaster may occur at any time of the day or night, weekend or holiday 
with little or no warning and may affect more than one jurisdiction. 
3. Local government officials, both elected and appointed, will, to the best of their abilities 
carry out all responsibilities regarding public safety and the protection of property.  These 
responsibilities include attention to all phases of emergency management and provisions 
to ensure the continuity of an effective, constitutional form of government. 
4. Disasters may affect citizens in a larger geographical area near Laramie County. 
Resources from all levels of government could be utilized to combat injury and death to a 
larger area. 
5. Incident management activities will be initiated and conducted using the principles of the 
Incident Command System (ICS) and the National Incident Management System (NIMS). 
6. Incidents could result in numerous injuries, fatalities, displaced victims, property loss, 
disruption of normal life support systems, essential public services and basic infrastructure 
and significant damage to the environment. 
7. Incidents could overwhelm the capabilities of the local first responder network causing the 
need for assistance from state and federal agencies. 
8. Some citizens will ignore, not hear, or not understand warning of impending disaster 
broadcast from media or local warning systems. 
9. Although warning systems may be operational they may not be heard by all citizens. 
10. Incidents are managed at the lowest possible geographic, organizational and jurisdictional 
level. 
 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
5 
 
Hazards and Vulnerabilities 
The following information and table is taken from the 2023 Wyoming Region 7 Hazard Mitigation 
Plan.  It shows risk and vulnerability for Laramie County and all its communities.   
Results and Methodology 
Using existing hazards data, plans from participating jurisdictions, and input gained through 
planning and public meetings, the HMPC agreed upon a list of hazards that could affect Laramie 
County and/or the City of Cheyenne.  Hazards data from FEMA, the Wyoming Office of Homeland 
Security (including the 2021-2026 Wyoming State Hazard Mitigation Plan), the National Oceanic 
and Atmospheric Administration, the Spatial Hazard Events and Losses Database for the United 
States (SHELDUS), and many other sources were examined to assess the significance of these 
hazards to the planning area.  The hazards evaluated in this plan include those that have occurred 
historically or have the potential to cause significant human and/or monetary losses in the future. 
In 2016 the State of Wyoming determined Mitigation Planning throughout the state would become 
regionalized and therefore combining all Region 7 hazard mitigation plans which include Laramie, 
Goshen, and Platte Counties.  Within the regional plan each county’s hazards are individually 
identified and identified as a whole for the jurisdiction.  Members of the HMPC used a hazards 
worksheet to rate the significance of hazards that could potentially affect the participating 
jurisdictions.  Significance was measured in general terms, focusing on key criteria such as the 
likelihood of the event, past occurrences, spatial extent, and damage and casualty potential.  Note 
that the significance of the hazard may vary from jurisdiction to jurisdiction.

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
6 
 
Disaster Declaration History 
As part of the hazard identification process, the HMPC researched past events that triggered 
federal and/or state emergency or disaster declarations in the planning area.  Federal and/or state 
disaster declarations may be granted when the severity and magnitude of an event surpasses the 
ability of the local government to respond and recover.  Disaster assistance is supplemental and 
sequential.  When the local government’s capacity has been surpassed, a state disaster 
declaration may be issued, allowing for the provision of state assistance.  Should the disaster be 
so severe that both the local and state governments’ capacities are exceeded; a federal 
emergency or disaster declaration may be issued allowing for the provision of federal assistance. 
The federal government may issue a disaster declaration through FEMA, the U.S. Department of 
Agriculture (USDA), and/or the Small Business Administration (SBA).  FEMA also issues 
emergency declarations, which are more limited in scope and without the long-term federal 
recovery programs of major disaster declarations.  The quantity and types of damage are the 
determining factors.  
A USDA declaration will result in the implementation of the Emergency Loan Program through 
the Farm Services Agency.  This program enables eligible farmers and ranchers in the affected 
county as well as contiguous counties to apply for low interest loans.  A USDA declaration will 
automatically follow a major disaster declaration for counties designated as major disaster areas 
and those that are contiguous to declared counties, including those that are across state lines.  
As part of an agreement with the USDA, the SBA offers low interest loans for eligible businesses 
that suffer economic losses in declared and contiguous counties that have been declared by the 
USDA.  These loans are referred to as Economic Injury Disaster Loans. 
Roles and Responsibilities 
A mayor, city representative, county commissioner or elected official, as a jurisdiction’s Chief 
Elected Official (CEO), is responsible for the public safety and welfare of the people of that 
jurisdiction. The local CEO is responsible for coordinating with Emergency Management and local 
resources to address the full spectrum of actions to prevent, prepare for, respond to, and recover 
from incidents involving all hazards including terrorism, natural disasters, accidents, and other 
contingencies dependent upon State and local law. A CEO has extraordinary powers to suspend 
local laws and ordinances, such as to establish a curfew, direct evacuations, and, in coordination 
with the local health authority, to order a quarantine. Officials may provide leadership and play a 
key role in communicating to the public, and in helping people, businesses, and organizations 
cope with the consequences of any type of domestic incident within the jurisdiction. Officials also 
negotiate and enter into mutual aid agreements with other jurisdictions to facilitate resource-
sharing; and requests State and, if necessary, Federal assistance through the Governor of the 
State when the jurisdiction’s capabilities have been exceeded or exhausted. 
Concept of Operations 
It is the responsibility of local governments to provide the necessary oversight and directions when 
assisting with the management of emergencies in order to protect life and property from the effects 
of hazardous events.  The Emergency Management Agency (EMA) is statutorily in place to assist 
all jurisdictions within Laramie County, and their CEO’s, with the development and implementation 
of a comprehensive plan for emergency operations.  EMA acts as the agent in leading the 
emergency management program; activating and applying the EOP as an operational and policy 
guide for any emergencies or disasters.   

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
7 
 
This plan is based upon the concept that emergencies begin and end at the local level.  The 
emergency support functions, performed by various groups responding to an emergency, will 
coordinate together under the Lifelines concept to ensure planning and response efforts are being 
met to the best of their ability.  All levels of emergency services will utilize both the “Incident 
Command System” (ICS) and the “National Incident Management System” (NIMS) to prepare for 
and respond to an emergency or disaster.  
A comprehensive emergency operations plan is concerned with all types of hazards that may 
develop in the community.  As shown below, it addresses a comprehensive approach to 
emergency management including the 5 Phases of Emergency Management. 
Mitigation:  Mitigation activities are those that eliminate or reduce the probability of a 
disaster occurring. It also includes those long-term actions that lessen the undesirable 
effects of unavoidable hazards. 
Preparedness:  Preparedness actions serve to develop the response capabilities needed 
in the event an emergency should arise.  Planning, training and exercises are among the 
activities conducted under this phase.  
Response:  Response is the actual providing of emergency services during a crisis.  
These actions help to reduce casualties and damage, and speed recovery.  Response 
activities include warning, evacuation, rescue, and similar operations.  
Recovery:  Recovery is both a short-term and long-term process. Short-term operations 
seek to restore vital services to the community and to provide the basic needs of the public. 
Long-term recovery focuses on restoring the community to its normal, or improved, state 
of affairs. Examples of recovery actions include restoration of non-vital government 
services and reconstruction in damaged areas.  The recovery period offers an opportune 
time to institute mitigation measures, particularly those related to the recent disaster.  
Protection:  The capabilities necessary to secure the homeland against acts of terrorism 
and manmade or natural disasters.   
When an emergency occurs and exceeds the capability of the local government response 
agency(s), EMA, with notification to the local CEO, will contact other local incorporated city/town 
policy groups and request available response resources in order to manage events.   When the 
emergency exceeds all resources within Laramie County, EMA is tasked to request support 
through neighboring EMA jurisdictions and/or the Wyoming Office of Homeland Security. If the 
events goes beyond the capabilities of the state, the state will then request support and resources 
from the federal government.  Federal assistance usually is extended to aid in recovery from 
major disasters.  
Direction and Control 
The final responsibility for all emergency management belongs to the Laramie County 
Commissioners and the elected officials of the jurisdiction(s) affected by the emergency. These 
officials are the decision making group for all policy level decisions and are the executive head of 
the emergency service coordinators and EOC staff.  During response operations, the members 
of the policy group will act in concert and advise/direct the activities of the entire response 
organization through the EOC emergency service coordinators.  They will also be available to 
constituents to address non-routine matters.  
The Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Management Director is responsible for coordinating 
the emergency management program.  They will make routine decisions and advise the Policy 
Group on alternatives when major decisions are required of that body.  During emergency 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
8 
 
operations, they are responsible for the proper functioning of the EOC and its staff.  The Director 
also acts as liaison with other local, county, state and federal emergency management agencies.  
Specific persons in departments/agencies are responsible for fulfilling their responsibilities as 
stated in this Base Plan, Lifelines/ESFs, and the Annexes thereto. Department supervisors will 
retain control of their employees and equipment during response operations. Standard operating 
procedures are required of each department having responsibilities in this plan. These SOPs must 
include:  
 
Recall of personnel during non-duty hours.  
 
Prioritization of tasks to guide recovery work.  
 
Procedures to be followed which deviate from normal.  
 
Specific emergency authorities that may be assumed by the designated successor during 
emergency situations.   
Local governments have the overall responsibility for the direction and control of event operations 
within their jurisdiction.  Local governments and state agencies should coordinate to ensure tasks 
and responsibilities are not assigned that conflict with other directives.   
During disasters or large emergencies, the County Commissioners and/or the elected officials of 
the affected jurisdiction can: 
 
Order the activation of the EOC. 
 
Coordinate with the Operations Section Chief, Emergency Manager, the EOC staff and 
the disaster site during emergency operations. 
 
Determine the level of EOC operations needed to mitigate the emergency. 
 
Prepare and issue a local disaster declaration prior to requesting outside assistance for 
restoration and recovery. 
During some periods of an emergency, department supervisors will be required to remain in the 
EOC and direct their departments from that facility.  During any large scale emergency, the EOC 
will in fact become the seat of county/city government for the duration of the crisis.  
Support positions for the elected officials are: 
 
Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Manager 
 
City/County Attorneys 
 
Public Information Officer 
 
City/County Risk Managers 
 
City/County Clerks 
 
Human Resources Manager 
 
City/County Treasurers 
Continuity of Government Operations 
Each agency is responsible for developing and maintaining an agency Continuity of Operations 
Plan (COOP). COOPs should provide standard operating procedures necessary for dealing with 
emergencies and disasters that could result in the following: 
 
Direct harm or impact to an agency’s personnel, facilities and equipment. 
 
Interruption and resumption of critical infrastructures of local government. 
 
Request for support to another impacted agency. 
 
Loss or damage to vital records. 
 
 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
9 
 
The COOP and supporting documents should include the following: 
 
Protection of personnel and property, including warning and evacuation and accounting 
for the whereabouts of personnel. 
 
Swift assessment and reporting to elected officials of the impact on personnel and 
facilities. 
 
Notification or recall of personnel. 
 
Line of Succession (see succession of leadership) 
 
Protection, proper storage, safeguarding, recovery and restoration of vital and important 
records and documents. 
 
Review, exercise, evaluate and update the agencies continuity of operations plan. 
 
Develop and maintain a list of agency resources that may be needed to carry out your 
assignment.  
Agencies will provide CLCEMA with names and contact information for the agency director and 
two alternatives.  
Each agency will retain its identity and autonomy through all phases of event operations. The 
Laramie County Commissioners have general control of the Cheyenne/Laramie County 
Emergency Management Agency and are responsible for carrying out the provisions of the 
Wyoming Homeland Security Act.  
Emergency Operations Center  
The Laramie County Emergency Operations Center (EOC) serves as a focal point for emergency 
management and coordination with, and between, governmental departments, ESF Agencies, 
and incident response personnel.   
The EOC is the physical or virtual location established (or designated) within the county from 
which Laramie County, Albin, Burns, City of Cheyenne, and Pine Bluffs. Representatives will 
coordinate information and resources to support incident management activities.  The EOC 
houses the staff, equipment, and communications technology that local leadership needs during 
emergency response.   
Facility  
The main Laramie County EOC is located in the Archer Complex Government Building at 3962 
Archer Parkway, Cheyenne, Wyoming.  A secondary/City of Cheyenne EOC is located at 415 W. 
18th Street, Cheyenne, WY 82001. 
The mobile command and communications vehicle can be operated as an EOC. 
Any building designated by any affected jurisdiction can be designated as an EOC. 
Function 
The Emergency Operations Center provides necessary space and facilities for the centralized 
direction and control of the following functions: 
 
Collecting, analyzing, and sharing information related to the incident  
 
Maintaining and reporting on situational status regarding the incident, including but not 
limited to, threats, impacts, trends, and response activities.  
 
Supporting requests, allocating, and tracking resources needed for response.  

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
10 
 
 
Developing plans, projecting current and future needs, and assuring successful 
demobilization of EOC operations.  
 
Determining policy direction through decision-making, direction, and coordination.  
Manning  
The Laramie County EOC will be manned by representatives of Laramie County, Senior Officials 
from the effected jurisdiction, the private sector, and emergency management volunteers involved 
in emergency operations and volunteers from civic organizations as appropriate. The Laramie 
County Information Technology Department will be activated for any emergency involving the 
activation of the EOC.  
Activation of EOC 
There are three functional levels of EOC activation based on the individual situation and need for 
coordinated response. The EOC is not staffed 24/7 for monitoring activities. CLCEMA staff 
members are responsible for the day-to-day monitoring, alert, and notification functions.  LCCCC 
or Incident Commanders directly contact CLCEMA on call staff members in the event of an 
incident where the EOC functional levels need to be activated or increased.   
The Emergency Management Coordinator will determine if and to what level the EOC should be 
activated. When the Emergency Management Director is not available, the CLCEMA on call staff 
member will make the determination.  EOC activation can be requested by the following 
authorities: Incident Commander, WOHS Director, or LCCCC.  EOC Level 1 activation can only 
be approved by CLCEMA in coordination with the Local CEO for the effected jurisdictions or the 
Laramie County Board of Commissioners.   
Additionally, the activation of the EOC may follow one of the following “trigger” events: 
 
LCCCC is overwhelmed with requests for resources and assistance to the extent they 
cannot maintain primary 9-1-1 Public Safety Answering Point (PSAP) capabilities. In this 
case, Dispatch will maintain PSAP responsibilities, while the EOC absorbs requests for 
resources from Incident Command. 
 
LCCCC cannot obtain incident response resources as requested by Incident Command. 
 
The CEO of the effected jurisdiction or the Laramie County Board of County 
Commissioners declare a State of Emergency. 
 
Protective action orders are issued for a large enough area that Incident Command 
requires additional resource support to implement the order. 
 
CLCEMA may activate the EOC for any specific event or partial opening for the purpose 
of information dissemination.  
Note:  All levels of EOC activation can be in person or virtual which is incident driven and 
determined by CLCEMA.  
 
 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
11 
 
EOC Activation Levels 
Level 3 – Monitoring/Enhanced Activation: Certain incidents that have the potential to rise to 
the level of an emergency or disaster. This activation is primarily supported by CLCEMA. This 
activation level may include one or more subject matter experts depending on the nature of the 
incident (e.g., hazardous materials spill) and may be activated virtually. The situation may require 
additional staff or equipment and supplies from another department or jurisdiction to deal with the 
incident.  CLCEMA will support at this level either from the CLCEMA office or on scene Command 
Post. CLCEMA staff in office will fill all EOC roles such as Situational Awareness Section, 
Planning Support Section, or Resources Support Section.  CLCEMA staff at the Command Post 
will assist in ICS positions such as Liaison, PIO, Planning, Logistics, and Finance/Admin if 
requested by the IC.

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
12 
 
Level 2 – Partial Activation: Emergencies or non-catastrophic disasters which have special 
characteristics requiring response by multiple municipal or county departments and partner 
agencies. This activation level requires the acquisition and/or use of special resources and will 
require support from selected departments.  CLCEMA will support at this level either from the 
CLCEMA office or on scene Command Post.  CLCEMA staff in office will either fill all EOC roles 
or bring in additional volunteers or EMA support to fill roles such as Situational Awareness 
Section, Planning Support Section, or Resources Support Section.  Additionally, CLCEMA staff 
at the Command Post will assist in ICS positions such as Liaison, PIO, Planning, Logistics, 
Finance/Admin.   
Figure 2:  Level 2 EOC Partial Activation Structure 
Level 1 – Full Activation: Extraordinary disasters which require the coordinated response of 
Laramie County, municipal departments, NGO’s, supporting agencies, neighboring jurisdictions, 
and the greater community to save lives and preserve and protect property. This level of activation 
will require 24-hour response coordination and utilization of staff from other county and municipal 
departments and agencies, state agencies, and federal partners.  
The EOC will initially be organized using the Incident Support Model (ISM), as illustrated in Figure 
3 below. For some EOC activations, not all positions or sections will be staffed. In these 
situations, EOC personnel may fill two roles (such as both Situational Awareness Section Chief 
and Planning Support Section Chief), but sections should not be combined or collapsed. The EOC 
Manager assumes all roles and responsibilities of non-activated/unfilled EOC positions.

SC-EOC Organizational Structure 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
14 
 
The EOC Director is responsible for: 
 
Setting EOC objectives 
 
Facilitating the development of policy direction and resource prioritization by elected 
officials and administrative leadership through the MAC Group 
 
Determining the need for additional command-level staff, including an Assistant Manager, 
Legal Advisor, Safety Officer, or other technical specialists 
 
Overseeing EOC operations and ensuring that objectives are being developed and carried 
out in coordination with on scene incident management objectives 
 
Managing coordination and communication with all activated EOC Sections and ESF’s 
The Public Information Officer (PIO) is responsible for: 
 
Ensuring timely, accurate, and accessible information is shared with the public 
 
Coordinating with subject matter experts to collect and develop appropriate public 
information and materials 
 
Reporting to the incident scene, as requested by an Incident Command (IC), to collect 
information regarding the incident from operational staff and/or to provide briefings to the 
media 
 
Distributing disaster messaging on all relevant platforms 
 
Developing messaging for designated spokespersons to share with the public and/or 
media, including elected officials 
 
Determine location and development of JIC/JIS operations 
The Situational Awareness Section is responsible for: 
 
Collecting, analyzing, and disseminating incident information through assessment of 
Community Lifelines  
 
Developing a Situation Report (SITREP) for each operational period 
 
Creating products for the MAC Group, PIO, and other internal and external stakeholders 
 
Monitoring weather and geologic forecasts and providing updates to the EOC, IC, and 
MAC Group, as appropriate 
 
Processing requests for information 
 
Developing reports, briefings, and presentation products 
 
Developing material to support public messaging 
 
Integrating Geographic Information Systems (GIS) and other technical information 
 
Maintaining updated displays in the EOC and other methods as appropriate 
The Planning Support Section is responsible for: 
 
Coordinating closely with the Incident Command Planning Section (if established) to 
ensure that both on-scene and EOC personnel have appropriate contingency plans 
 
Providing a range of current and future planning services including development of an 
EOC plan for support of the incident 
 
Developing an EOC Support Plan (ESP) and other incident reports as needed 
 
Developing contingency, deactivation, and recovery plans 
 
Assisting in developing and executing the shared goals of multiple jurisdictions and 
organizations involved in managing the incident 
 
Coordinating a standard planning process to achieve the objectives of the EOC leadership 
and foster unity of effort among all organizations represented 
 
 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
15 
 
The Resource Support Section is responsible for: 
 
Processing resource and task requests from on-scene ICs and/or their designated 
Logistics Section Chiefs 
 
Enhancing, but not replacing, pre-existing resource management structures such as 
dispatch centers, expanded dispatch, or agency-specific mutual aid agreements 
 
Sourcing, requesting/ordering, and tracking to their assigned incident all resources, 
including supplies, equipment, and personnel, acquired from: 
o Government departments and agencies 
o Community organizations and nongovernmental partners 
o Mutual aid agreements 
o Purchased or leased items 
The Center Support Section is responsible for: 
 
Coordinating EOC facility and staff support needs including communications, IT, and 
administrative processes  
 
Organize general services such as supplies, equipment, facility security, maintenance, 
staff support, and scheduling 
 
Conducting financial tracking, documentation, and filing for the EOC 
The Multiagency Coordination Group (MAC) is responsible for:  
 
The lead Chief Elected Official for the effected jurisdiction or the Laramie County Board of 
Commissioners are responsible for the formulating of policy and operational guidelines for 
the conduct of emergency operations.   
 
They are also responsible for the overall management of survival and recovery efforts.  
 
Emergency Management Director:  The Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency 
Management Director is charged with planning; organizing, directing and supervising 
emergency operations conducted within Laramie County and will: 
o Assign and, where necessary, train personnel to accomplish required tasks in the 
operation of the EOC. 
o Ensure that the EOC and appropriate Annexes are periodically updated. 
o Maintain sufficient supplies and equipment to ensure the operational capability of 
the EOC. 
o Supervise and coordinate the functions during operations. 
o Provide briefings as needed. 
o Perform such other tasks as may be required to safeguard property and protect 
the people of Laramie County during emergencies. 
o Locate and coordinate resources and resource requests. 
Being said that all incidents are handled at the lowest possible level beginning with local response 
and Incident Command, the EOP may not be utilized or effective for all emergencies. As the size 
of the incident expands the EOP can be utilized to enhance both incident command and the ties 
to local government. The EOP organizational structure recognizes both site specific IC and EOC 
Operations Incident Command. The EOP can be utilized at any time to enhance IC decisions at 
any location. 
 
 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
16 
 
Lifelines - ESF 
The Emergency Support Functions, Lifelines, and Core Capabilities are utilized by EOC staff to 
support the overall operations of an incident.  Together, these functions provide an invaluable tool 
for incident and EOC personnel to understand the overall situational awareness of incident in 
order to efficiently respond, recover and mitigate the situation.   
An Emergency Support Function (ESF) leader will be appointed for each ESF needed during an 
event. The leader or designee will be the primary communications link with or within the 
Emergency Operations Center (EOC). This approach will maintain the needed command 
structure between the command center(s) and the agency providing support to the ESF.  The 
ESF section of this EOP provides more information on each of these functions and how they are 
to be implemented. 
Message Control 
1. Radio communications received in the EOC will be via the Laramie County Combined 
Communications Center or ARES communications.  All emergency traffic through the 
center is recorded on digital format and in the Computer Aided Dispatch System. 
2. Incoming information will be passed to the Resource Support Section for information and 
disposition as deemed appropriate. 
3. Actions taken as a result of incoming information will be made as an entry in the computer. 
4. Outgoing Messages: Outgoing messages will be given to the Resource Support Section 
for distribution.  Copies of messages directing the commitment of resources or personnel 
will be sent to the Operations Section Chief at the On-Scene Command Post or Incident 
Commander Designee.  All information passed will be copied to the Emergency 
Management Director by the agency directing the action.  All messages are to be 
documented using the ICS 213 Form for Resource Requests.  
Succession of Leadership  
The line of succession for continuity of government for Laramie County is as follows:  
1. County 
a. Chairman, County Commissioners 
b. Vice Chair, County Commissioners 
c. County Commissioner 
d. Laramie County Sheriff 
e. Laramie County Clerk 
2. Cities/Towns 
a. Mayor 
b. President of City Council 
c. Vice President of City Council 
d. Chairperson of Finance Committee 
e. Chairperson of Public Services Committee 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
17 
 
3. Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Management Agency 
a. Director of Emergency Management 
b. Operations Coordinator/Fire Warden 
c. Planning/Administration Coordinator 
Line of succession for each agency/department head is according to the department rules and/or 
standing operating procedures established by each department. 
Agency heads will determine to what degree their assigned personnel to the EOC will be 
authorized to make decisions and commit or expand department resources. Agency heads shall 
retain responsibility for the direction and control of their respective operations and personnel and 
shall coordinate all department actions related to the incident with the EOC when in operation.  
Public Information and Warnings  
Purpose 
This section establishes personnel assigned and an effective alert and warning system 
within Laramie County capable of disseminating adequate and timely warning to the 
county officials and public in the event of threatened disaster.  
Emergency Public Information 
Laramie County or any jurisdiction within the county should attempt to identify and appoint a 
person who can serve as a Public Information Officer (PIO), if not on a day-to-day basis, at least 
in time of emergency.  A working knowledge of media in the following areas should be addressed. 
 
Basic knowledge of media and print operations. 
 
Basic knowledge of emergency preparedness responsibilities. 
 
Perform the PIO duties as their primary function during an emergency. 
 
React calmly in emergency situations. 
 
Ability to coordinate with different levels of operations, jurisdictions and other information 
officers.  
During an emergency the PIO serves as the spokesman for the elected official(s) in charge of the 
emergency. The PIO is a member of the EOC staff. The primary function is to coordinate and 
gather information, and disseminate emergency information to the media. In the absence of a PIO 
the elected official(s) for the affected jurisdiction(s) should serve as the point of contact for media.  
The PIO is a member of the command staff and works directly with the EOC commander. The 
EOC is responsible for the prompt, correct dissemination of information.  
The PIO Should: 
 
Attend as many briefings and situation updates as possible, regardless what section of 
the EOC is present. 
 
Establish a list of all media, including such items as hours of operation, power output, 
coverage area and details on backup power. 
 
Maintain contact with all media sources as frequently as possible to ensure credibility, to 
ensure the media list is current, and to demonstrate the need for media help in time of 
emergency. 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
18 
 
 
During any emergency, establish a “Joint Information Center” (JIC) to assure smooth 
relationships with other PIOs and to release the same information.  
 
Establish an information office which is the official point of contact for the media during an 
emergency.  
 
Develop a media distribution plan to determine the quickest, most efficient way to get 
emergency information to all media sources. 
 
Maintain a news release file 
 
Keep commanders and officials apprised of media needs and releases. 
 
Collect, verify, and disseminate information to the public that will help them make 
decisions about their health, safety and welfare. 
 
Understand existing plans and procedures of the “Joint information System (JIS) and JIC. 
 
Seek legal counsel prior to releasing potentially dangerous information.  
Joint Information System (JIS) 
The JIS is a network of PIOs working together to deliver accurate, timely information the public 
needs and wants. PIOs can use any variety of methods to talk or provide information with other 
PIOs. The system should ensure clear, accurate information is being delivered amid the confusion 
of disaster response. No one person is in charge of the JIS, it is a cooperative effort. The JIS 
mirrors the ICS structure, so the primary responding agency will most likely take the lead in 
releasing information. The JIS gives the PIOs the ability to share information, resources and 
equipment designed to enhance response ability. 
Joint Information Center (JIC) 
The JIC is a central location to facilitate operation of the JIS during and after the event. The JIC 
enhances information coordination, reduces misinformation and maximizes resources.  The JIC 
is a physical location with tools to enhance the flow of public information. It provides a central 
working facility where PIOs can gather. The JIC allows PIOs to handle increased information 
needs by the media and the public. The JIC can provide “one-stop shopping” for the media to get 
official information. A JIC can be as simple as a small office or the corner of a command center 
or as complex as a pre-established facility with multiple phone lines, full media monitoring and 
news conference stage. 
Media Resources 
Broadcast Media 
Both radio and TV broadcast stations at the local level will play a critical role in the timely 
dissemination of information to the citizens within the county. Immediate routing of information is 
essential to the health and welfare of the citizens. It is important to continuously brief and up-date 
media with critical information.  This form of media will be a necessary partner in the beginning 
stages of any emergency. 
Published/Newspaper Media 
All newspapers, monthly, weekly, or daily that are published in your county will play a crucial role 
in information dissemination.  Local print media can be used to document events and provide 
victim recovery information. Print journalists write comprehensive stories that include analysis of 
the event and a chronology of proceedings leading up to, causing and immediately following the 
event.  

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
19 
 
Web-Based & Social Media 
Web-Based and social media outlets want the same information as other media outlets. Most web 
based organizations are attached to more traditional news outlets, but more strictly web-based 
news outlets are appearing.  Social Media outlets such as, Twitter, Facebook, Instagram, 
YouTube, and TikTok are quick resources to reach many people within a short period of time.  
Not all local residents or businesses may be utilizing all of these resources; posting information 
on all is a best practice.  
Emergency Warnings  
The Laramie County Combined Communications Center (LCCCC) is the 24 hour warning point 
for Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Services. The Wyoming Highway Patrol Dispatch 
Center is also a 24 hour warning point.  The warning point will receive reports of emergencies 
from all routes used for normal operation including the NWS and the NAWAS from the Wyoming 
Highway Patrol, phone, email, texting, social media, fax, etc. The priority of the LCCCC is public 
notification of an immediate threat to life and property in a timely manner. 
Radio, pager, and notification will be used to notify all emergency responders under the control 
of the LCCCC. Notification will be made to all other public warning points. The communications 
center will notify and coordinate local first and primary responders in any disaster or major 
emergency.  CLCEMA will work in coordination with LCCCC and WHP Dispatch to assist in 
dissemination of public emergency notifications.   
For major events such as matters of national security or imminent danger to Laramie County or 
the region, notification could come directly from the State of Wyoming via the Wyoming Office of 
Homeland Security or directly from the Federal Government.  CLCEMA, if given warning will notify 
the Laramie County Commissioners and any other city/county officials as needed.   
For severe weather watches and warnings, these will be initiated by the National Weather Service 
(NWS) with follow-up by CLCEMA who will assist with further public notifications and senior official 
notifications to the affected areas.

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
20 
 
Notification Routes 
Outdoor Warning System 
The Laramie County Outdoor Warning System uses a steady warning signal to alert citizens of 
immediate threat to life and property. The tone is an all hazards alert intended to notify citizens to 
seek any EAS affiliated form of radio / TV media for instructions. The system consists of multiple 
sirens located in Laramie County, including Cheyenne, Burns, Albin and Pine Bluffs.  During 
tornado warnings the Outdoor Warning System is activated automatically via the computer system 
and is based on the NWS polygon identifying the location of the tornado.   
Emergency Alert System (EAS) 
EAS allows broadcast stations, satellite radio, cable systems, DBS systems, participating satellite 
companies, and other services to send and receive emergency information quickly and 
automatically, even if their facilities are unattended. EAS was designed so that if one link in the 
dissemination of alert information is broken, members of the public have multiple alternate 
sources of warning. EAS equipment also provides a method for automatic interruption of regular 
programming, and is able to relay emergency messages in any language used by the EAS 
participant. 
An Emergency Manager may access and use the system, via the IPAWS program, to send out a 
public warning.  The National Weather Service can also assist in accessing the EAS system to 
assist local and state jurisdiction with all hazard notifications.   
Rave Alert Emergency Communications  
Rave Alert is an internet based critical notification system used to mass notify citizens in Laramie 
County of potential dangers.  The map based system can isolate a neighborhood or notify the 
entire county of timely critical information.  The LCCCC along with Cheyenne/Laramie County 
Emergency Management has the ability to use Rave to rapidly reach both emergency services 
and citizens.   
Integrated Public Alert and Warning System (IPAWS) 
IPAWS uses different pathways to simultaneously send alerts through many different channels 
such as televisions, radios, cell phones, wireless emergency alert, internet applications, electronic 
billboards, social media and other electronic pathways.  This system is activated either by a 
Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Management staff member or by the Duty Officer with the 
Wyoming Office of Homeland Security.  
Wireless Emergency Alerts (WEA) 
Wireless Emergency Alerts (WEA) allows public safety officials to send warnings directly to cell 
phones and other mobile devices in affected areas. These short messages look like text 
messages, but unlike texts, which are sent directly to a phone number, these warnings will be 
broadcast to all phones within range of designated cell towers. The alerts will tell residents of the 
type of warning, the affected area, and the duration.  

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
21 
 
Amateur Radio Communications   
Amateur Radio Operators often referred to as “Ham Radio” can be utilized for emergency public 
information.  Their ability to relay information to local, national or worldwide outlets can be of great 
use in an emergency especially if the event becomes long term and other sources of information 
sharing is lost due to the incident.  Networks can easily be set up by volunteer operators to pass 
information rapidly.  
Other communication methods include: 
 
Cable Interrupter/Override System 
 
Mobile siren and public address systems by emergency vehicles 
 
Emergency services radio and pager systems.  
 
Telephone 
 
Personal Contact  
 
Radio Amateur Civil Emergency Services (RACES) 
 
Door to door 
 
Other means as deemed appropriate 
Emergency and Disaster Assistance Process 
This section describes the process, templates, and individuals involved in issuing a declaration of 
emergency or disaster for all-hazards incidents, including potential coordination with the state and 
federal governments. A formal declaration of emergency or disaster may expedite access to 
necessary resources and assistance from neighboring communities, key stakeholders, and when 
appropriate the state or federal government.  
 
There are two types of declarations assistance can be provided for in the Stafford Act, Emergency 
declarations and major disaster declarations. Both types provide the President to provide 
supplemental federal disaster assistance however, the events related to the two different types of 
declaration and scope and amount of assistance differ.  In both situations local, state, and federal 
agencies will conduct assessments of the emergency to determine qualifications of declaration 
and resources or assistance available.  Not all large emergencies or disasters will qualify for 
assistance.  
Stafford Act Definitions of Declarations 
Emergency Declaration: Is more limited in scope and without the long-term Federal recovery 
programs of a major disaster declaration. Generally, Federal assistance and funding are provided 
to meet a specific emergency need or to help prevent a major disaster from occurring. 
Major Disaster Declaration: Provides more federal programs for response and recovery than an 
emergency declaration.  Unlike an emergency declaration, a major disaster declaration may only 
be issued after an incident.  
 
 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
22 
 
Locally Declared Emergencies or Disasters 
If an emergency exceeds the resources and capabilities of local response operations and/or 
immediately available mutual aid resources, the Laramie County Board of County Commissioners 
may declare a state of emergency. This declaration may include the entire county or specific 
communities, as requested by City/Town Councils. The declaration can be used to request and 
secure additional assistance, including the Wyoming State Response Coordination Center 
(SRCC).   
A template for a local disaster declaration, provided by Wyoming Office of Homeland Security 
(WOHS) or the Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA), is available through CLCEMA. 
Once the declaration is sent to WOHS, a State of Emergency can be declared by the Governor 
of Wyoming, as described below.   
Emergency Declaration 
 
EOC activities are increased with Emergency Management evaluating Lifelines and 
coordinating with ESF’s.  
 
Anticipates that the situation can be resolved with Town/County resources and limited 
mutual aid.  
 
Informs the public that it may not be “business as usual” in the jurisdiction until the 
emergency is resolved. This could apply to curfews, rationing, availability of government 
services, traffic routing, etc.  
 
CLCEMA, in coordination with local officials, are authorized to request and employ all 
available resources and personnel of the Town/County as needed.  
 
Informs local government employees that elected officials may need to temporarily 
suspend Town/County personnel policies regarding temporary hires, remote work, 
adjusting standard hours of operation, changing duty stations, and more for some or all 
employees in response to the emergency.  
Disaster Declaration  
 
Includes everything for an Emergency Declaration with the exception that the jurisdiction 
does not have adequate local or immediately available mutual aid resources.  
 
Includes a request for assistance from the next-higher level of government. For example, 
the Town of Pine Bluffs can make a Disaster Declaration to Laramie County County asking 
for specific resources to assist with an emergency. Laramie County can make a Disaster 
Declaration to the State of Wyoming.  
 
If a situation is in both the Town and the County, it is recommended that the two bodies 
make a joint Disaster Declaration to the State. 
Local Declaration Considerations  
 
An Emergency Declaration does not need to be made as a pre-requisite to creating a 
Disaster Declaration.  
 
The Emergency or Disaster Declaration may extend to all or any geographic area within 
Town or County jurisdiction.  
 
All Emergency/Disaster Declarations should be made in writing.  
 
A joint Town/County Declaration formalized by resolution is preferred.  

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
23 
 
 
It is preferred that the Town first makes a Disaster Declaration to the County. This process 
will improve the likelihood of receiving state assistance. However, nothing prevents the 
Town from making a Disaster Declaration directly to the State.  
 
Once the Local Disaster Declaration is forwarded to Wyoming Office of Homeland Security 
(WOHS), a State of Emergency can then be declared by the Governor of Wyoming using 
an executive order or proclamation. CLCEMA shall maintain supporting documentation for 
the Disaster Declaration through damage assessment and ESF information. In many 
cases, implementation of the Wyoming State Response Coordination Plan (SRCP) and 
access to state and federal programs or funding are contingent on correctly following the 
declaration process in a timely manner.  
State Declared Disasters 
If the Governor determines the disaster is beyond the ability of local government to effectively 
respond, a State of Emergency can be declared through an executive order or proclamation.  
 
CLCEM will provide documentation in support of a Declaration request, in accordance with 
Wyoming State Statute and WOHS policy.  
 
The action of the Governor will be in support of the local jurisdiction's expressed needs.  
The Declaration of a State of Disaster or State of Emergency by the Governor serves to:  
 
Activate the emergency response, recovery, and mitigation phases of the state and local 
emergency management plans, including the SRCP.  
 
Provide authority for the mobilization and deployment of all resources to which the plans 
refer, or any other provision of law relating to emergencies.  
 
In alignment with the SRCP, allow various state agencies authority and responsibility for 
emergency functions.  
WOHS will present the Governor with the following information:  
 
Nature and amount of state and local resources that have been or will be committed  
 
An estimate of the amount and severity of damage and impacts  
 
An estimate of the type and amount of state assistance needed  
The State of Disaster or Emergency shall continue until the Governor finds that the threat of 
danger has passed or that the disaster has been dealt with to the extent that emergency 
conditions no longer exist, and the Governor terminates the State of Disaster or Emergency by 
executive order or proclamation. The executive order shall indicate:  
 
The nature of the disaster  
 
The area threatened  
 
The conditions which have brought it about, or which make possible termination of the 
State of Disaster or Emergency 
An executive order or proclamation shall be disseminated promptly by means calculated to bring 
its contents to the attention of the public and, unless the circumstances attendant upon the 
disaster prevents or impede, shall be promptly filed with WOHS, the Secretary of State, and the 
local jurisdiction in the area to which it applies. 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
24 
 
Federal Assistance 
As established in the Robert T. Stafford Disaster Relief and Emergency Assistance Act (Stafford 
Act), all requests for a Declaration by the President that a major disaster exists shall be made by 
the Governor. The Stafford Act includes two types of disaster declarations: 
 
Emergency Declarations: The President can declare an emergency for any occasion or 
instance when the President determines federal assistance is needed. Emergency 
declarations supplement state and local efforts in providing emergency services. 
 
Major Disaster Declarations: The President can declare a major disaster for any natural 
event that the President determines has caused damage of such severity that it is beyond 
the combined capabilities of state and local governments to respond. A major disaster 
declaration provides a wide range of federal assistance programs for individuals and 
public infrastructure, including funds for both emergency and permanent work.  
Federal assistance through a Presidential Disaster Declaration is requested by the Governor if 
the situation meets the criteria for a declaration. The Governor submits a written request to the 
president through FEMA Region VIII in Denver, CO. FEMA gathers information to supplement the 
Governor's request, and this is sent to the President who determines the final disposition. The 
disposition of the request is transmitted through FEMA Region VIII back to the Governor. FEMA, 
part of the Emergency Preparedness and Response Directorate of the Department of Homeland 
Security, is tasked with coordinating the response of federal agencies with state and local 
governments. 
Based on the Governor's request, the President may declare that a Major Disaster or Emergency 
exists, thus activating an array of federal programs to assist in the response and recovery effort. 
Not all programs, however, are activated for every disaster. The determination of which programs 
are activated is based on the needs found during the damage assessment and any subsequent 
information that may be discovered. 
FEMA disaster assistance falls into three general categories:  
 
Individual Assistance — aid to individuals and households;  
 
Public Assistance — aid to public (and certain private non-profit) entities for certain 
emergency services and the repair or replacement of disaster-damaged public facilities; 
 
Hazard Mitigation Assistance — funding for measures designed to reduce future losses 
to public and private property.   
Some declarations will provide only individual assistance or only public assistance. Hazard 
mitigation opportunities are assessed in most situations. Procurement Disaster Assistance Teams 
(PDAT) may be available as well to support in the disaster recovery process.  CLCEMA in 
coordination with WOHS will provide specific rules, regulations, and templates to local officials 
and the public if a declaration should be approved.

Plan Development and Maintenance 
The development process of the EOP has included participation of key local and state agencies.  
The plan continues to be refined and revised as needed through a process of yearly review, 
exercise and evaluation by emergency management and the agencies that function under the 
plan. The plan is updated based on the evolving changes in emergency operations locally, within 
the state, and at the federal level.  Changes are verified and built to be in line with the National 
Preparedness Goals and in coordination with the National Response Framework (NRF) and the 
National Incident Management System (NIMS).  The Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency 
Management Agency shall provide updated electronic or hard copies of any revision made to the 
plan.   
The contents of this plan must be known and understood by those people responsible for its 
implementation.  The Cheyenne/Laramie County EMA Director will brief staff members and 
county officials concerning their role in emergency management and the contents of this plan in 
particular. Department directors are responsible for development and maintenance of their 
respective segments of this plan and their appropriate supporting Standard Operating Procedures 
(SOPs) or Response Plans as stated herein and set forth in each Annex.  
The Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Management Agency will ensure a bi-annual review 
of this plan is conducted. When minor updates have been added, the approval of the EOP will be 
submitted to the Laramie County Commissioners only.  Every six years or when major updates 
have been added, the approval of the EOP will be submitted to all of the Laramie County 
governing bodies.  The EMA Director will coordinate this review and any plan revision found 
necessary. Testing and exercise of the plan will occur at least once a year in the form of a 
simulated emergency exercise in order to provide practical, controlled experience to those 
emergency managers tasked within the plan.

27 
 
The Wyoming Office of Homeland Security as the lead emergency program within the State of 
Wyoming requires each local emergency management coordinator to produce and adhere to an 
Emergency Operations Plan, created and established for each jurisdiction. 
By resolution the Board of Laramie County Commissioners adopted National Fire Protection 
Association Standard 1600 (NFPA 1600), the Standard on Disaster/Emergency Management and 
Business Continuity Programs 2000 Edition. NFPA 1600 requires under chapter 3-4.1 that 
Laramie County shall implement a mitigation plan to eliminate hazards of mitigate the effects of 
hazards that cannot be eliminated. Further, NFPA 1600 3-4.2 provides that the mitigation strategy 
shall be based on the result of hazard identification and risk assessment, impact analysis, 
program assessment, operational experience, and cost benefit analysis.  
 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
28 
 
 
 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
29 
 
Introduction 
The events of 2017 challenged the Federal Emergency Management Agency’s (FEMA) ability to 
effectively balance concurrent large-scale disaster operations. Hurricanes Harvey, Irma, and 
Maria, as well as wildfires in California, required FEMA to allocate scarce resources across four 
FEMA regions, from the west coast of the United States to the Caribbean Sea. The 2017 
Hurricane Season FEMA After-Action Report identified the need to create a new operational 
prioritization and response construct, which would effectively characterize incidents and identify 
the root causes of priority issue areas. This new construct aims to create outcome-driven, 
effective solutions and help distinguish the highest priorities and most complex issues from other 
incident information. In 2018, FEMA operations along the Atlantic coast, the Florida Panhandle, 
Alaska, and the Western Pacific, including a historic wildfire season in California and additional 
operations in Hawaii validated the pressing need for a way to prioritize response operations. 
FEMA established the community lifelines during 2018 to address this need and has continued to 
improve and refine its implementation based on lessons learned.  The interrelationship of ESFs, 
core capabilities, and lifelines can be thought of in terms of means, ways, and ends and are 
described in the following paragraphs. 
Emergency Support Functions (The Means) 
The Emergency Support Functions (ESF) Assistance Guide outlines specific tasks or functions 
that may be carried out prior to, during and after an emergency or a disaster. The guide describes, 
in specific detail, the operational system identified in the Laramie County Emergency Response 
Plan (ERP), that will be used to carry out the tasks or functions listed. It should be based on the 
Incident Command structure described in the basic plan.  
These tasks mirror the emergency support functions of the Wyoming Response Plan and can 
also be applied to specific functions within the Incident Command System (ICS) or the National 
Incident Management System (NIMS).  
These functions are described in a checklist format for ease in times of stress and the possibility 
that a support function may be led by a person unfamiliar with the duties of the support function 
or the ERP.  
The Federal Government and many state governments organize their response resources and 
capabilities under the ESF construct. ESFs have proven to be an effective way to bundle and 
manage resources to deliver core capabilities. The Federal ESFs are the primary, but not 
exclusive, Federal coordinating structures for building, sustaining, and delivering the response 
core capabilities. The ESFs are vital structures for responding to Stafford Act incidents; however, 
they may also be used for other incidents. Most Federal ESFs support a number of the response 
core capabilities. Though not listed under every ESF, all ESFs support the core capabilities of 
Planning, Operational Coordination, and Public Information and Warning.  In addition, there are 
responsibilities and actions associated with Federal ESFs that extend beyond the core capabilities 
and support other response activities, as well as department and agency responsibilities.   
The Federal ESFs bring together the capabilities of Federal departments and agencies and other 
national-level assets. ESFs are not based on the capabilities of a single department or agency, 
and the functions for which they are responsible cannot be accomplished by any single 
department or agency. Instead, Federal ESFs are groups of organizations that work together to 
deliver core capabilities and support an effective response.  
Cheyenne/Laramie County ERP – Emergency Support Functions 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
30 
 
The ESFs and other organizing bodies are the way departments and agencies, community 
organizations, and industries organize to enhance coordination and integration to deliver the 
response core capabilities. 
As noted above, many state and local jurisdictions have adopted and tailored the ESF construct. 
Since state and local jurisdictions establish ESFs based on their specific risks and requirements, 
there is no mandatory or direct linkage to the Federal ESFs. State and local governments are 
encouraged to engage members of the whole community as part of whatever coordinating 
processes they use. 
Lifelines (The Ends) 
The National Response Framework (NRF), 4th Edition, introduces community lifelines.  The 
primary objective of lifelines is to ensure the delivery of critical services that alleviate immediate 
threats to life and property when communities are impacted by disasters.  Stabilizing lifeline 
services is the highest priority when responding to disasters.  The construct organizes and aligns 
these critical services into one of seven lifelines, which help frame the way disaster impacts are 
identified, assessed, and addressed. 
 
The lifelines provide an outcome-based, survivor-centric frame of reference that assists 
responders with the following: 
 
Rapidly determining the scale and complexity of a disaster; 
 
Identifying the severity, root causes, and interdependencies of impacts to critical lifesaving 
and life-sustaining services within impacted areas; 
 
Developing operational priorities and objectives that focus response efforts on the delivery 
of these services by the most effective means available; 
 
Communicating disaster-related information across all levels of public, private, and non-
profit sectors using a commonly understood, plain language lexicon; and 
 
Guiding response operations to support and facilitate their integration across mission 
areas. 
Each lifeline is composed of multiple components and subcomponents that help define the 
services that make up that lifeline.  The lifelines and components are fixed categories, but the 
subcomponents may be adjusted as necessary.  The table below shows the lifelines and their 
components.
