Purpose 
The purpose of this annex within the Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan is 
to provide an overview of the roles, responsibilities, and coordinating mechanisms to manage 
hazardous materials incidents within Laramie County, Wyoming. Laramie County encompasses 
the City of Cheyenne and the towns of Albin, Burns, and Pine Bluffs, all of which face potential 
risks due to the diverse hazardous materials utilized, manufactured, stored, and transported within 
its boundaries. 
Given the location of Cheyenne at the intersection of major transportation routes such as 
Interstate 25, Interstate 80, the Union Pacific Railroad, and the Burlington Northern Railroad, the 
possibility of hazardous materials incidents involving these routes and affecting local communities 
is a real concern. Additionally, pipeline systems crisscross the county, and significant hazardous 
materials manufacturing facilities, including the Holly Frontier Refinery and Dyno Nobel, further 
elevate the risk profile. 
 
 
Annex C - Hazardous Materials  

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
97 
 
The implications of an accidental hazardous materials release—whether to the air, land, or 
surface/subsurface waters—are significant, posing immediate and long-term threats to both the 
population and the environment. In response, it is imperative that all efforts to control and mitigate 
the consequences of such incidents align with the numerous federal acts, codes, standards, and 
rules governing hazardous materials. The National Response Framework (NRF) and the National 
Incident Management System (NIMS) serve as overarching frameworks guiding our response 
strategies. 
Each agency involved retains the responsibility for developing and implementing documents that 
establish policies, standard operating procedures, or operating guidelines. These documents 
must align with the roles and responsibilities outlined within the Cheyenne/Laramie County 
Emergency Response Plan and its respective annexes.  
Objectives 
1. Minimize Hazards: Develop and implement a plan of action designed to minimize hazards 
to life, property, and the environment resulting from the release of hazardous materials. 
2. Coordinated Response: Establish robust procedures for the notification, alerting, 
warnings, and reporting of incidents. This ensures a coordinated and efficient effort in 
response to hazardous materials releases. 
3. Resource Identification: Identify and catalog emergency response organizations, 
equipment, and additional resources that can be mobilized to respond effectively to 
hazardous materials incidents. 
4. Integration: Provide a mechanism for the seamless integration of community and facility 
response procedures into the broader county emergency management plan. This 
facilitates a unified and well-coordinated response. 
5. Responsibility Delineation: Clearly delineate the responsibilities of local government, 
facility owners/operators, and their respective relationships in the context of response and 
recovery operations. This includes planning and exercise operations, ensuring clarity and 
efficiency in our emergency management efforts. 
Responsibilities and Governing Principles 
1. Leadership and Coordination: The primary responsibility is to establish effective 
leadership, coordination, and unified on-scene command of emergency response forces 
in the event of a hazardous materials emergency. This ensures a cohesive and strategic 
approach to managing the incident. 
2. Diverse Response Organizations: Recognizing the diverse nature of hazardous materials 
emergencies, the plan acknowledges the potential involvement of various on-scene 
response organizations. This includes emergency services personnel from all levels of 
government, industry representatives, private contractors, and the media. The complexity 
of such emergencies may necessitate specialized equipment and technical knowledge, 
and critical decisions must be made in areas such as release containment, emergency 
worker safety, public protective actions, and environmental protection. 
3. Unified and Integrated Response: While response organizations are typically trained to 
operate within their agency command structure, the plan emphasizes the rare but crucial 
need for a unified and integrated multi-organizational response, especially in major 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
98 
 
incidents. The implementation of a strong command and control structure is deemed 
essential for effective collaboration. 
4. Hierarchy of Direction and Control: The direction and control of the response begin at the 
local level but may expand to involve city, county, state, and federal agencies. This 
hierarchical approach ensures a scalable and coordinated response that can adapt to the 
evolving nature and severity of the hazardous materials incident. 
Assumptions 
1. Rapid identification of unknown hazardous materials is crucial. 
2. Hazardous material releases immediately threaten human health or the environment, 
necessitating prompt responses. 
3. Evacuation may be necessary based on release severity, protecting the public and 
emergency responders. 
4. Specialized equipment and expertise are essential for handling hazardous materials, 
especially chemical, biological, radiological, or nuclear substances. 
5. Releases can result in injuries or casualties, requiring medical response capabilities. 
6. Immediate and long-term environmental effects demand monitoring and remediation 
efforts. 
7. Coordinated responses from multiple agencies at local, regional, and national levels are 
required. 
8. Effective communication within and outside responding agencies is essential. 
9. Adherence to federal, state, and local regulations influences response strategies. 
10. Hazardous material releases can cause significant social, economic impacts, disrupting 
normal activities. 
11. Identifying the source and cause of releases may be challenging initially. 
Response 
In formulating an effective emergency response, obtaining key initial information is essential for 
proper notification and prioritization of incidents. The following considerations guide this process: 
1. Immediate Threat Assessment:  
 
Assess immediate threats to civilians and responders.  
 
Evaluate actions that can be taken without further endangering lives. 
2. Risk of Delayed Action:  
 
Assess potential loss of lives due to delayed action. 
3. Incident Progression:  
 
Monitor incident spread or intensity increase. 
4. Protection of Resources:  
 
Identify high-value resources at risk.  
 
Evaluate possibility of saving or salvaging resources. 
5. Health, Economic, and Environmental Impact:  
 
Consider short and long-term impacts on health, economy, and environment. 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
99 
 
6. Resource Availability:  
 
Determine availability and reasonable cost of required resources. 
Life safety remains the top priority, adopting a defensive response strategy and considering 
factors like positioning, isolation, and facing away from the incident. 
First-In Responder - Establish Command: The initial actions of the "first-in" responder, 
including scene size-up, reporting to dispatch, and implementing initial isolation measures, 
significantly influence the incident's outcome. Reference the Emergency Response Guidebook, a 
crucial tool aiding first responders in rapidly identifying hazards and ensuring public and self-
protection during the initial response phase. 
Initial Response to Hazardous Materials Incidents: 
 
Responder Agencies: 
o Involves law enforcement, fire, and EMS as the minimum initial responders. 
o Responsibility for implementing the Incident Command System lies with the first 
responding agencies. 
 
Response Levels: 
o Small incidents may be handled by responders trained at Awareness and 
Operations levels. 
o Larger incidents necessitate the immediate involvement of Hazardous Materials 
Technicians, subject matter experts, additional agencies, and personnel. 
 
Unified Command and Isolation Measures: 
o Establish Unified Command for effective coordination. 
o Expand isolation zones and distances as needed. 
 
Coordination Responsibilities: 
o Law enforcement and public works agencies collaborate on cordons, road blocks, 
and overall incident management. 
A comprehensive response strategy, involving prompt assessment, prioritization, and 
coordination, ensures an effective and safe resolution to hazardous materials incidents. 
Additional Agency Notifications 
In the event of hazardous materials incidents, a systematic approach to additional agency 
notification and incident command is crucial for effective emergency response. The following 
outlines the key components of this process: 
Additional Agency Notification: 
 
Local Jurisdictional Involvement: 
o Local Hazardous Materials Team activation. 
o Notification to Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Management Agency, 
involving elected officials and collaboration with the Wyoming Office of Homeland 
Security. 
 
Regional and State Involvement: 
o Coordination with the Regional Emergency Response Team-Region 7 (RERT-7). 
o Engagement of the 84th Civil Support Team. 
o Involvement of regulatory bodies such as the Wyoming Department of 
Environmental Quality and the Wyoming Department of Health. 
 
 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
100 
 
 
Local Healthcare Partners: 
o Collaboration with Cheyenne-Laramie County Health Department, Cheyenne 
Regional Medical Center, and Cheyenne VA Medical Center. 
o Ensuring the safety of the water supply and prevention of sewer contamination 
through coordination with the Board of Public Utilities (BOPU). 
 
Mutual Aid Requests: 
o Provision for additional Hazardous Materials Technicians through Mutual Aid 
requests with F.E. Warren A.F.B. Fire Department, Wyoming Air Guard Fire 
Department, Holly Frontier Refinery, and Railroad Emergency Response Teams. 
 
Operational Considerations: 
o Anticipation of the need for multiple relief rotations in and out of the "Hot Zone." 
Incident Command Post Options: 
 
Strategic Placement: 
o Options include on-scene Incident Command Vehicle, HAZMAT 7-CF&R 
Command Center, EMA "Interagency Command Vehicle," and Cheyenne Police 
Department Enforcement Command Vehicle. 
Public Warning Systems: 
 
Public Communication Strategies: 
o Utilization of Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Management Agency or the 
Laramie County Combined Communications Center for public warnings. 
o Activation of Outdoor Warning Sirens, NOAA Weather Radio, Emergency Alert 
System (radio and television broadcasts), IPAWS, and law enforcement patrol 
officers for neighborhood public address broadcasts and door-to-door contacts. 
Evacuation and Shelter-in-Place Protocols: 
 
Coordination and Management: 
o Evacuation coordination through law enforcement, public works, mass transit 
providers, and the American Red Cross. 
o Residents may be advised to evacuate or shelter in place. 
Notification of Major Events: 
 
State and Federal Communication: 
o Reporting of events of major significance to the Wyoming Office of Homeland 
Security for subsequent notification to applicable federal agencies. 
Containment Strategies:  
Containing a hazardous material release is of utmost importance to prevent further contamination, 
injury, and damage while stabilizing the scene. Employing confinement methods such as 
damming, diking, absorption, patching, plugging, closing valves, and covering is critical. The 
establishment of control zones (hot, warm, cold) and corridors minimizes exposure risks. 
 
 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
101 
 
General Guidelines: Guidance for hazardous materials containment procedures underlines the 
following priorities: 
 
Defensive Posture: Control the material defensively. 
 
Confined Area: Constrain the material to as small an area as possible. 
 
Prevent Container Failure: Take measures to prevent container failure. 
 
Containment Techniques: Utilize various methods like closing valves, plugging holes, 
repositioning containers, etc. 
 
Facility Operations: Exercise caution when operating or closing valves at fixed facilities, 
ensuring actions align with the facility owner/operator's directives. 
Decontamination Protocols: The level of decontamination is tailored to the agent and exposure 
level, considering: 
 
Likelihood of Exposure: Emergency decontamination for situations where exposure is 
likely but not confirmed. 
 
Known Contamination: Graduated responses based on known contamination and skin 
contact/irritation. 
Clean Up and Recovery:  
Clean-up and recovery is the final stage of hazardous materials incident response. Since a 
shipper, spiller, or owner is responsible for recovery and clean-up, HAZMAT team and other 
response personnel will not participate in this stage of an incident, unless incidental cleanup 
mitigates the problem. This decision will be made by the HAZMAT supervisor. 
It is imperative that responsible parties in hazardous materials incidents are identified. The 
responsible party is liable for clean-up operations. Responsible parties should be informed that 
they are financially responsible for the clean-up and disposal of the hazardous materials. There 
are a wide range of rules and regulations pertaining to clean-up and disposal, and Hazardous 
Materials Technicians, and others, should be well versed in notification requirements. 
Clean-up operations will be conducted under the supervision of the appropriate authority having 
jurisdiction. This jurisdiction may include city, county, state and/or federal agencies including, but 
not limited to: health or environmental agency representatives. 
Reports and Documentation:  
Post-incident critique, investigation reports, and after-action reports are mandatory for all 
hazardous materials incidents. These reports may be submitted to the Wyoming Office of 
Homeland Security, the State Emergency Response Commission (SERC), and the Local 
Emergency Planning Committee (LEPC). Tracking incident expenses is vital for reimbursement 
purposes, emphasizing the collaborative effort in controlling, containing, and mitigating the 
incident. 
EOC Activation 
ESF Duties under Level 1 EOC Activation 
In the event of Level 1 activation at the CLC-EOC, the CLC-EOC Manager will initiate the 
activation of all 15 Emergency Support Functions (ESFs). As the situation unfolds, the staffing 
levels at the CLC-EOC may be adjusted, either increased or decreased, based on the scale, 
complexity, or duration of the incident or event. Refer to the Emergency Operations Plan (EOP) 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
102 
 
Base Plan, specifically the section on EOC Activation, for a breakdown of all activation levels and 
the associated procedural steps. 
ESF Duties under Level 3 or 2 EOC Activation 
For an activation Level of 3 or 2 of the Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Center 
(CLC-EOC) due to a HazMat incident, the following ESFs are expected to be activated:  
 
ESF #01 (Transportation): Initiates temporary road closures and alternate routes as 
needed  
 
ESF #03 (Public Works): Assesses potential impacts to the public drinking water supply 
or other public infrastructure  
 
ESF #04 (Firefighting): Provides personnel, equipment, and resources for response, as 
needed   
 
ESF #05 (Information & Planning): Gathers information about the incident and identifies 
resources required to respond   
 
ESF #07 (Resource Support): Locates and provides supplies to emergency response 
personnel, as needed 
 
ESF #08 (Public Health and Medical Services): Coordinates protective actions for 
medical staff, first responders, and impacted populations; deploys medical resources to 
the incident, as needed  
 
ESF #10 (Hazardous Materials): Identify and mitigate hazardous material releases 
 
ESF #11 (Agricultural, Natural, and Cultural Resources): Assesses potential impacts 
to natural resources, wildlife, water sources, and the environment  
 
ESF #13 (Public Safety and Security): Enforces protective actions and provides safety 
and security around the incident site  
 
ESF #15 (External Affairs): Provides accurate updates and messaging to the public 
about protective actions, road closures, or other potential impacts 
Primary ESF:  
ESF #10 should consider the following for HazMat incident response:   
 
Ensure proper HazMat response protocols are being followed.   
 
Ensure that an IC and ICS has been adequately established at the incident, including 
development of a written Incident Action Plan (IAP).   
 
The local HazMat response agency with authority is responsible for detection, 
assessment, and monitoring of the spill site.   
 
Coordinate with ESF #08 (Public Health and Medical Services) to activate and deploy 
medical resources to the incident, as well as determine protective actions for staff, first 
responders, and impacted populations.   
 
Develop and issue protective actions for the public and distribute in coordination with ESF 
#15 (External Affairs) through the Joint Information System (JIS). 
 
Coordinate with ESF #11 (Agriculture, Natural, and Cultural Resources) to assess 
potential impacts to natural resources within or near the incident site, as well as implement 
mitigation actions for further contamination.   
 
Anticipate needs for additional response resources, including RERTs, CSTs, WY DEQ, 
and EPA Environmental Response Teams (ERT).

 
Provide information, personal protective actions, signs and symptoms, and other relevant

Signs and symptoms of contamination   
 
Ongoing incident operations 
 
Confirm jurisdictional communication strategies to prevent any misinformation sharing. 
 
 
 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
105 
 
 
Situation 
Description / Definition  
In various emergency scenarios, such as floods, hazardous material spills, radiological threats, 
volcanic activity, major fires, dam breaches, or similar incidents, Laramie County emergency 
responders and EOC personnel may decide to evacuate all or part of the community to minimize 
potential loss of life. 
Regardless of the specific threat, the evacuation approach remains consistent. The initial step 
involves identifying the at-risk area and weighing the risks of evacuation against those of 
sheltering in place. Managers then designate routes to low-risk areas, arrange transportation for 
those without private means, open and staff buildings for housing and feeding evacuees, and 
provide clear instructions to the public. 
Historically, during emergencies with advance warning, over 50% of residents in threatened areas 
evacuate before official orders. Studies suggest that 80% of evacuees prefer seeking shelter with 
friends or relatives over designated public facilities. If this trend continues, Laramie County may 
need to accommodate up to 17,000 people in case of a total evacuation. 
For this plan, evacuation involves removing individuals from the threat's path before impact, falling 
under the jurisdiction of law enforcement and public safety as outlined in this plan. Rescue, on 
the other hand, involves moving victims from an impacted area and follows departmental standard 
operating procedures. 
The authority to order an evacuation lies with local government or the governor, with the elected 
chief executive officers in Laramie County holding the formal authority. In on-scene operations, 
the Incident Commander (IC) assumes this authority. According to Wyoming statute, no one can 
force citizens to evacuate against their will unless they obstruct emergency operations. However, 
officials can enforce evacuation of public facilities or businesses, and law enforcement can take 
action against those violating established perimeters or interfering with responders. 
Evacuation Considerations 
1. Identification of Need: 
a. Not all emergencies necessitate public protective action or evacuation. The 
Incident Commander (IC) must assess the risk of leaving the population 
unprotected against the risks associated with sheltering in place or evacuation. 
2. Identification of the Area Affected (Reference: Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency 
Response Plan): 
a. Before implementing an evacuation, the following steps must be taken: 
i. Identify high hazard areas, including those potentially impacted if the 
incident escalates or conditions change. 
ii. Identify 
potential 
evacuation 
routes, 
considering 
capacities 
and 
vulnerabilities to the hazard. 
1. Primary evacuation routes for Laramie County include I-80, I-25, 
US 85, US 30, WY 210 (Happy Jack Road), and WY 216 (Albin 
Hwy). 
Annex D - Emergency Evacuation 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
106 
 
2. Coordinate the use of routes if the evacuation involves multiple 
jurisdictions. 
b. Alert and warn the public at risk, providing specific information about the risk, 
proactive actions required, and potential risks of non-compliance. 
3. Determination of Time Needed for Evacuation: 
a. Utilize the following formula to estimate the time needed to evacuate a threatened 
area: 
i. TD (Time from response to decision to evacuate) + TA (Time needed to 
alert and instruct the public) + TM (Time needed to mobilize the population) 
+ TT (Time needed to leave the hazard area) = TN (Total time needed to 
evacuate). 
ii. Special Note: Under ideal circumstances, 2500 vehicles can pass a single 
point in one hour. Estimate four persons per vehicle. 
iii. For example: 
1. If it takes the IC 15 minutes to decide to evacuate (TD = 15), 25 
minutes to activate warning and notification (TA = 25), 30 minutes 
for the population to mobilize (TM = 30), and 15 minutes for each 
vehicle to clear the hazard area (TT = 15), then the total time 
needed to evacuate is 1 hour and 25 minutes (TN). 
Special populations 
The Access and Functional Needs (AFN) population within Laramie County that may need 
assistance during evacuation include students, nursing homes, assisted living centers, hospitals, 
physically and mentally challenged residents, and the Laramie County Detention Facilities. 
Juvenile and foster care facilities are the responsibility of the business or the State of Wyoming 
Department of Family Services.  All of the identified institutions are required to have internal 
disaster plans which address evacuation. In addition, the EOC may assist in procurement of 
specialized transportation resources such as ambulance and vehicles for handicap transportation. 
The Access and Functional Needs (AFN) program within Laramie County helps teach and provide 
resources to people who are not able to care for themselves during a disaster.  Under EOC 
direction, the ESFs will need to consider transportation, housing, food and shelter options for the 
AFN population. 
EOC Activation 
ESF Duties under Level 1 EOC Activation 
In the event of Level 1 activation at the CLC-EOC, the CLC-EOC Manager will initiate the 
activation of all 15 Emergency Support Functions (ESFs). As the situation unfolds, the staffing 
levels at the CLC-EOC may be adjusted, either increased or decreased, based on the scale, 
complexity, or duration of the incident or event. Refer to the Emergency Operations Plan (EOP) 
Base Plan, specifically the section on EOC Activation, for a breakdown of all activation levels and 
the associated procedural steps. 
ESF Duties under Level 2 or 3 EOC Activation 
For an activation Level of 2 or 3 of the Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Center 
(CLC-EOC) due to a Emergency Evacuation Incident, the following ESFs are expected to be 
activated:

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
109 
 
Appendix A 
Emergency traffic policy 
 
All law enforcement/public safety employees not directly involved in the management of 
the incident shall be available for traffic control and direction. 
 
The chief elected official has the authority to close roads and to restrict access to and from 
all areas of the affected jurisdiction(s). 
 
Law enforcement/public safety has the authority to remove stalled and parked vehicles 
which impede the flow of traffic. 
 
Traffic flow and direction may be altered, reversed, etc. at the direction of the IC. 
IC will coordinate changes in traffic flow with law enforcement/public safety through the EOC.

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
111 
 
Appendix C 
Evacuation Protocols 
General 
Under Wyoming statute, no law enforcement officer or public safety official has the legal right to 
impose a mandatory evacuation order on citizens in their own homes. Citizens should be informed 
of the need to leave a hazardous area and the possible consequences of not leaving. Citizens 
who obstruct the evacuation process may be arrested. 
The protocols cover door to door, siren alert, and the mobile public address process. These 
methods may be conducted alone or in combination or in conjunction with the Emergency 
Broadcast System (EBS), Cable Interrupt System, and through IPAWS. 
Door to Door 
Door to door contact is an effective but time and labor intensive method of alerting, warning and 
evacuating an area. More detailed information can be shared with the population and positive 
confirmation can be made that the individual received the warning, understood the instructions 
and knew the consequences of their actions. 
Emergency information to provide: 
 
Type of incident 
 
Expected duration 
 
Available time to evacuate 
 
Recommended actions 
 
Implication of not following recommended actions. 
 
Neighborhood congregation points for citizens needing transportation. 
 
Evacuation routes 
 
Reception and shelter locations 
Conduct door to door evacuations 
 
Attempt to contact residents  
 
Present evacuation information 
Reporting 
 
Notify IC as areas or blocks are cleared. 
 
Notify IC number of residents not evacuating 
Mobile Public Address 
Mobile public address is more effective than door to door contact but you are only able to convey 
a limited amount of information.  It is most effective used in combination with an emergency 
broadcast system, Cable Override and door to door contact.  It may be used to alert and warn the 
public prior to the request to evacuate or to advise of protective actions short of evacuation. 
 
 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
112 
 
 
Objective 
The Nuclear/Radiological Incident Annex aims to serve as a comprehensive guide tailored for 
local governments and agencies, facilitating effective coordination and providing essential 
guidelines for public safety personnel engaged in preparedness activities. This strategic 
document specifically addresses Nuclear/Radiological Incidents, encompassing accidents, 
terrorism, and sabotage. Additionally, it establishes a framework for seamless collaboration 
between state agencies and local jurisdictions in responding to incidents involving radioactive 
materials. The Annex delineates clear responsibilities and ensures the coordination of local 
agencies within their jurisdictions, fostering collaboration with neighboring jurisdictions for a 
cohesive and efficient response to incidents involving radioactive materials. 
Scope 
The local response to a specific incident is contingent upon various factors such as the local 
resources' capacity for response, the type and quantity of materials involved, the potential impact 
on the environment, and the size of the affected area. In the event of an incident, licensees, local 
jurisdictions, and the State of Wyoming share responsibilities concerning response and recovery 
efforts. This Annex serves as a dynamic framework to guide decision-making and actions, 
ensuring a well-coordinated and effective response to Nuclear/Radiological Incidents. 
Shipper/Carrier/Licensee 
The shipper, carrier, and licensee must be in compliance with all federal, state, and local 
regulations. They are responsible for notifying proper authorities of an incident, providing 
expertise and shipping information to public safety and response personnel; providing equipment 
and personnel to conduct clean-up and recovery of the incident site; and reimburse response 
agencies as provided by law. 
Local Jurisdictions 
The “local jurisdiction” is defined as that local governmental entity which authorizes, regulates, or 
is otherwise deemed responsible for the health, welfare, and protection of residents and their 
property within the geographical boundaries in which a radioactive material incident occurs. 
Local jurisdictions have the primary responsibility for performing emergency response functions. 
Local emergency management personnel, law enforcement officials, fire department officials, and 
emergency medical technicians/paramedics will provide their usual range of emergency services 
for an incident involving radioactive materials. This includes the responsibility for having personnel 
trained in radiological protection which would include scene assessment; application of protective 
measures (time, distance, and shielding); the use of instrumentation; maintaining a current roster 
of trained personnel, and maintaining an adequate quantity of radiological instruments/equipment. 
 
 
Annex E - Nuclear/Radiological Incident  

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
113 
 
In many cases, shippers/carriers/licensees may not be able to exercise their responsibilities 
quickly enough to protect the public from the consequences of a radiological incident. The local 
jurisdiction must be prepared to effectively initiate life-saving and protective measures. This 
includes, but may not be limited to:  
 
Emergency planning  
 
Information gathering and exchange 
 
Situation analysis 
 
Evacuation and shelter of persons threatened 
 
Rescue and medical care 
 
Initiating and supporting radiological monitoring activities 
 
Fire fighting 
 
Access/egress control 
 
Public information 
 
Directing protection actions and decontamination when recommended by appropriate 
technical authorities 
 
Making notifications to other local and state authorities 
Cheyenne/Laramie County 
Cheyenne and Laramie County recognizes that as a regional transportation hub for both road and 
rail and the placement of a military installation, it may be more susceptible to an accident involving 
radioactive materials. 
As provided by state law, the Wyoming Highway Patrol is designated as the “Incident 
Commander” for all incidents involving radioactive materials on highways and interstates. Laramie 
County, its first responders, and HazMat teams rely on State Radiological Services to assist in 
response and determination of leaks and radiation levels.  
Concept of Operations 
All emergency services within Laramie County use the NIMS/ICS system to manage and 
coordinate response to any type of radiological incident. 
Consequence Management 
Consequence management focuses on mitigating the impact of an incident on both lives and 
property. It encompasses a range of measures designed to safeguard public health and safety, 
restore crucial government services, and offer emergency relief to individuals, businesses, and 
governments affected by the incident. FEMA serves as the Lead Federal Agency for consequence 
management. The development of this guide is focused on providing information and instructions 
to effectively address consequence management in incidents involving radioactive materials, 
whether they occur during transportation, within fixed facilities, or as a result of terrorism. 
 
 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
114 
 
Incident Type and Classification 
Incident Type:  For the purpose of this guide, there are three types of incidents involving 
radioactive materials. They are: 
 
Transportation Incident:  An incident which occurs in association with any activity 
involving the movement of radioactive materials by a motorized conveyance on roadways, 
to include trucks, trains, plains, automobiles, etc.  (This does not include movement of 
materials at a facility by forklift, hand-truck or other transfer method. Such an incident 
would be considered a fixed facility incident.) 
 
Fixed Facility Incident:  An incident which occurs in association with any activity involving 
radioactive materials at a fixed location. This would include industrial sites (uranium 
processing), temporary work sites (oil well injections, non-destructive testing of welds, 
etc.), manufacturing sites (thickness gauges, etc.), or any other location which does not 
involve the movement of radioactive materials by a motorized conveyance on roadways 
as described in #1a above. 
 
Terrorism Incident:  An incident which occurs in association with any deliberate act of 
sabotage or destruction which includes the use of radioactive materials. This type of 
incident may include transportation or fixed facility but, due to the initiating event, will 
require coordination of response actions to ensure crime scene issues are considered. 
Incident Classification:  There are also three classifications for incidents involving radioactive 
materials. They apply to each type of incident described above and are as follows: 
 
Level I: An incident in which no release of radioactive material has occurred. This is 
determined by visual assessment of the incident scene. If there is not a high confidence 
level by the response personnel in declaring a Level I incident, it should default to a Level 
II incident. 
 
Level II:  An incident in which there may be a release of radioactive materials. This is 
determined by visual assessment of the incident scene. Level II would be declared when 
there is reasonable doubt to the integrity of the containment of the radioactive materials 
(package shows significant damage, but there is no visible sign of material release). 
 
Level III:  An incident in which there is a release of radioactive materials. This is 
determined by visual assessment of the incident scene. There is a high level of confidence 
by the response personnel in declaring a Level III incident. 
Incident Response Phases 
Phase I – Actions:  This phase includes Notification, Classification, and Activation: 
1. WOHS Notification - This component of Phase I begins when the initial call is received 
by public safety officials. If first responders determine there is a radiological aspect to the 
incident, or that information is supplied during the initial call, a request for assistance can/is 
made through Cheyenne/Laramie County EMA and/or the WOHS Duty Officer for state 
assets such as WOHS Radiological Services, RERT 7, and 84TH Civil Support Team. 
2. Incident Classification - The on-scene responder shall initiate the classification of the 
incident based on their assessment of the scene. If a notification has been made to the 
WOHS Duty Officer, personnel or responding team(s) will make contact with appropriate 
personnel at the scene to determine the status of the incident and obtain additional 
information if possible. If the information provided indicates a need to change the 
classification, the responding team(s) and the on-scene personnel (preferably the Incident 
Commander) will make that change cooperatively.   

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
115 
 
3. Activation - If assistance beyond verbal information is requested or determined 
necessary, notified team(s) will load out applicable equipment and supplies and respond 
to the incident scene. If it is determined that additional personnel or specialties 
(environmental, health, etc.) are required to support the incident, those notifications will 
be made by responding team(s) at this time. The Incident Commander will be notified 
when responding team(s) depart for the incident and an estimated time of arrival (ETA). 
This same information will be requested from all other support personnel activated to 
support the response. Upon departure for the incident scene, the next phase of the 
response will begin. 
Phase II – Response:  This phase begins upon departure from the appropriate responding team’s 
facility. While en-route, additional information may be obtained from the scene or other resources, 
additional notifications made, and a review of operational options can be discussed. 
1. Upon arrival at the incident scene, team personnel should provide proper identification to 
on-scene personnel and check-in with the Logistics and/or the Incident Commander. The 
team lead should request an update briefing by the IC to determine the current status of 
the situation, other hazards present and current action plan. The team lead should provide 
a review of capabilities and recommendations for addressing the radiological aspect of the 
incident. The best option should then be chosen in a cooperative manner by all response 
personnel as appropriate. 
2. Additional visual assessments and information gathering may be required prior to any 
entry into the incident scene. Appropriate level of personal protection with IC approval 
shall be utilized. Entry activities should be conducted in a timely manner. All information 
gathered and the resulting follow-up recommendations should be provided to the 
command personnel. All activities within the incident scene should be considered based 
on a risk-benefit outcome. Scene assessment should focus on determining impacts on the 
health of responders, victims, and the public and the potential for environmental abuse. 
Further actions, such as bioassays, evacuation/sheltering, and recovery requirements 
should be based on this assessment.  Provide radiological monitoring equipment, to 
include survey instruments, dosimetry, diagnostic instruments, sampling supplies, and 
basic anti-contamination equipment (Personal Protective Equipment- PPE). 
Phase III – Recovery:  Recovery options should be presented to the command personnel and 
coordinated with recovery personnel (provided by shipper/carrier). If recovery capabilities of the 
shipper/carrier prove unacceptable to ensure public well-being, IC in coordination with SMEs may 
undertake appropriate options to ensure the incident scene does not result in a negative public 
health impact. SMEs and other support personnel will also insure that site recovery is to pre-
incident conditions. 
EOC Activation 
ESF Duties under Level 1 EOC Activation 
In the event of Level 1 activation at the CLC-EOC, the CLC-EOC Manager will initiate the 
activation of all 15 Emergency Support Functions (ESFs). As the situation unfolds, the staffing 
levels at the CLC-EOC may be adjusted, either increased or decreased, based on the scale, 
complexity, or duration of the incident or event. Refer to the Emergency Operations Plan (EOP) 
Base Plan, specifically the section on EOC Activation, for a breakdown of all activation levels and 
the associated procedural steps. 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
116 
 
ESF Duties under Level 3 or 2 EOC Activation 
For an activation Level of 3 or 2 of the Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Center 
(CLC-EOC) due to a Nuclear/Radiological incident, the following ESFs are expected to be 
activated:  
 
ESF #01 (Transportation): Initiates temporary road closures and alternate routes as 
needed 
 
ESF #02 (Communications):  Protection, restoration, and sustainment of national cyber 
and information technology resources 
 
ESF #03 (Public Works): Assesses potential impacts to the public drinking water supply 
or other public infrastructure  
 
ESF #04 (Firefighting): Provides personnel, equipment, and resources for response, as 
needed   
 
ESF #05 (Information & Planning): Gathers information about the incident and identifies 
resources required to respond   
 
ESF #07 (Resource Support): Locates and provides supplies to emergency response 
personnel, as needed 
 
ESF #08 (Public Health and Medical Services): Coordinates protective actions for 
medical staff, first responders, and impacted populations; deploys medical resources to 
the incident, as needed  
 
ESF #10 (Hazardous Materials): Oil and hazardous materials (chemical, biological, 
radiological, etc.) response 
 
ESF #11 (Agricultural, Natural, and Cultural Resources): Assesses potential impacts 
to natural resources, wildlife, water sources, and the environment 
 
ESF #12 (Energy): Energy infrastructure assessment, repair, and restoration Energy 
industry utilities coordination Energy forecast 
 
ESF #13 (Public Safety and Security): Enforces protective actions and provides safety 
and security around the incident site  
 
ESF #15 (External Affairs): Provides accurate updates and messaging to the public 
about protective actions, road closures, or other potential impacts 
Primary ESF:  
ESF #10 should consider the following for Nuclear/Radiological incident response:   
 
Ensure proper Nuclear/Radiological response protocols are being followed.   
 
Ensure that an IC and ICS has been adequately established at the incident, including 
development of a written Incident Action Plan (IAP).   
 
The local Nuclear/Radiological response agency with authority is responsible for 
detection, assessment, and monitoring of the spill site.   
 
Coordinate with ESF #08 (Public Health and Medical Services) to activate and deploy 
medical resources to the incident, as well as determine protective actions for staff, first 
responders, and impacted populations.   
 
Develop and issue protective actions for the public and distribute in coordination with ESF 
#15 (External Affairs) through the Joint Information System (JIS). 
 
Coordinate with ESF #11 (Agriculture, Natural, and Cultural Resources) to assess 
potential impacts to natural resources within or near the incident site, as well as implement 
mitigation actions for further contamination.   
 
Anticipate needs for additional response resources, including RERTs, CSTs, WY DEQ, 
and EPA Environmental Response Teams (ERT). 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
117 
 
Relevant ESF’s: 
ESF #01 should consider the following for a Nuclear/Radiological incident response:   
 
Assess the transportation impacts from the spill, including location, duration, and the need 
for alternate routes.   
 
Provide information on road closures and alternate routes for distribution to the public in 
coordination with ESF #15 (External Affairs) for distribution through the JIS.   
 
Address the need for evacuation planning and the identification of evacuation routes 
ESF #02 should consider the following for a Nuclear/Radiological incident response: 
 
Coordination with telecommunications and information technology industries 
 
Restoration and repair of telecommunications infrastructure  
 
Protection, restoration, and sustainment of national cyber and information technology 
resources 
ESF #03 should consider the following for a Nuclear/Radiological incident response: 
 
Infrastructure protection and emergency repair  
 
Infrastructure restoration  
 
Engineering services and construction management  
 
Emergency contracting support for life-saving and life-sustaining services 
ESF #04 should consider the following for a Nuclear/Radiological incident response:  
 
Mobilize resources, including personnel and equipment, to support decontamination.  
 
Address potential fire danger at incident site.  
ESF #05 should consider the following for a Nuclear/Radiological incident response:   
 
Develop and distribute reports on the status of operations and impacts, including Situation 
Reports as needed.   
 
Ensure the timely warning of Laramie County residents and visitors of potential or actual 
impacts, including relaying the IC’s issued protective actions  
 
Assess the need for sheltering or mass care services based on protective action orders.  
 
Organize information shared through other ESFs to establish situational awareness of 
impacts across the community, including human health, environmental damages, or

 
Develop a process for information sharing between local incident management agencies 
and other appropriate sectors that can offer support.   
 
Ensure proper hazardous materials notifications are made to the WOHS Duty Officer, 
SERC, WY DEQ, and NRC, as necessary.  
ESF #07 should consider the following for a Nuclear/Radiological incident response:   
 
Assist with specialized Nuclear/Radiological resource requests such as RERTs, CSTs, 
and EPA ERTs.  
 
 

Cheyenne/Laramie County Emergency Operations Plan 
 
118 
 
ESF #08 should consider the following for a Nuclear/Radiological incident response:  
 
Organize any necessary care or protective actions among hospitals, medical personnel, 
emergency service providers, and technical specialists.  
 
Ensure proper lockdown and decontamination procedures are initiated immediately upon 
notification of an incident.   
 
Provide public health response activities such as EMS triage, treatment, and 
transportation or evacuation of impacted individuals.  
 
Monitor the health and safety of first responders and healthcare staff.   
 
Coordinate with impacted drinking water supply entities, ESF #03 (Public Works), and 
ESF #11 (Agriculture, Natural, and Cultural Resources) on the issuance of boil water 
orders, do not drink orders, or other protective actions around contaminated drinking 
water.   
 
Provide information, personal protective actions, signs and symptoms, and other relevant 
human health information regarding the incident for distribution to the public in 
coordination with ESF #15 (External Affairs) for distribution through the JIS.  
ESF #11 should consider the following for a Nuclear/Radiological incident response:   
 
Assess potential impacts to natural resources during initial incident response and advise 
on immediate protective measures.  
 
Provide appropriate response actions to conserve, rehabilitate, recover, and restore 
natural, cultural, and historic resources impacted by the incident.  
 
Assess rural drinking water impacts from the spill in coordination with ESF #08 (Public 
Health and Medical).  
 
Monitor air quality levels in the area surrounding the incident site and initiate response to 
heightened levels of pollutants.  
ESF #12 should consider the following for a Nuclear/Radiological incident response: 
 
Energy infrastructure assessment, repair, and restoration  
 
Energy industry utilities coordination  
 
Energy forecast 
ESF #13 should consider the following for a Nuclear/Radiological incident response:   
 
Provide access control and security to impacted areas.   
 
Inform, initiate, and enforce protective actions, including evacuations as needed.  
ESF #15 should consider the following for a Nuclear/Radiological incident response:   

